# do-math as it stands on the evening of 25 November 2026: context for the third verification run

## 1. Why this document exists and how to read it

On 25 November 2026 the user asked, for the third time that day, for a multi-round verification run over the skill kept at `.claude/skills/do-math/`. In such a run several agents (repairers) each read a part of the skill in full and propose concrete corrections, other agents (judges) judge each correction against the actual files, the accepted corrections are applied, and the round repeats. One session, here called the driving session, sends the agents out and applies what they accept. The agents who do this work start with nothing: they have not seen the conversations in which the skill was designed, they do not know what the user has decided, and they do not know what else is running on the machine. This document gives them that knowledge. It rests on investigations made before each of the three runs: of the commit history, of the memory notes that accompany each commit, of the transcripts of the sessions in which the user and several AI sessions worked on the skill, of the earlier verification runs of the same skill, of the research instances that the skill is carrying on this machine, and, for this run, of where the bulk of the skill lies and what kind of bulk it is.

The two earlier runs of the day answered the same request and repaired 255 defects (section 10). They did not make the skill smaller; it grew. The user then gave the request again and added what this run is to put first: "Most important goal should be reducing bloat in docs, in a way that does not sacrifice its intricate details and value, with freedom in selection of means to achieve it" (section 3 quotes the request in full). The present run is built around that sentence. What it asks of a repairer is different from finding defects: to say the same thing in less, and to show that nothing was lost. Sections 13 to 15 exist for that work. They give what five investigators measured on the evening of 25 November: how large each guide, test module and program module is and how it came to be so, which passages are repeated where, what kinds of padding the guides hold and how much, what the tests cost, and what in the program is more than its job needs.

### What to read

Everyone reads sections 1 to 4, 5.4, 6 and 16. Sections 2 to 4 say what do-math is and what the user has decided; a correction that goes against a decision is rejected however well it is made. Section 5.4 says which remains of removed mechanisms are still in use. Section 6 says what is running from the very files the run edits. Section 16 lists structural changes that the run has adopted and that later rounds carry out. Sections 17 and 18 were added on 26 November, between round 1 and round 2, after the user had said how repetition among the guides is to be restructured and which knowledge research sessions do not need: section 17 gives a design of the guides that research sessions receive, and section 18 the knowledge that leaves them. Sections 19 and 20 were added between round 3 and round 4, when rounds 2 and 3 had shown that rewording gains little in the operator's documents and in the instructions of the two commands: section 19 gives a design of the operator's documents, and section 20 a design of the instructions the two commands give their sessions. Section 13 gave where the bulk of the guides lay when the run began; rounds 1 to 4 have dealt with most of what it lists, and the designs of sections 17, 19 and 20 have taken its place as the lead for a repairer of a guide. From round 5 the guides that research sessions receive are three assignments where they were six, and the numbers of the assignments after 15 have changed with that. Beyond that, each assignment of the freeze document reads the following.

From round 7 each repairer also reads the file of findings that falls to it, `임시/do-math_검증_20261125-3/평가/영역_<k>.md`, where `<k>` is the repairer's number; a repairer for whom no such file exists has no finding left. The findings come from an evaluation of the skill that the user had another session make on 26 November. Section 21 says what that evaluation was, how its findings were sorted and which repairer has which; the freeze document (영역 배분, 「평가의 발견」) says how a finding is treated.

### What kind of statement each sentence is

The document mixes statements of different kinds, and a reader who is about to rely on one of them needs to know which kind it is.

A **user decision** is something the user said, or something put to the user that the user answered or approved. Where the user's own words exist they are given, in the language the user used, with an English gloss where the words are Korean, and with their source: the session and the time of the message, or the memory note that records it. Times of messages are UTC. Times of commits and of events on the machine are KST, nine hours ahead, and are marked so. An answer of one word to a particular question or item is a decision on that item. An approval of a long proposal as a whole is a decision too: the agreement of twenty-five decisions for the fourth generation, the forty criteria of 7 November and the agreement for the finalize pipeline were worded by an assistant and approved by the user, and they bind. Where an approved text and the user's own words differ on one matter the user's words govern, and a later decision governs an earlier one. A user decision binds the run.

An **assistant's choice** is something an AI session decided that the user was not asked about, or was told of and did not answer. Much of the detail of the skill is of this kind: the particular way in which a decision of the user was carried out in code or in wording. Such a choice does not bind the run. It may be revisited where there is a reason, within everything the user did decide. In section 4 a statement is a user decision when it quotes the user, gives the time of the user's message or the memory note that records it, cites one of the forty criteria by its letter and number, or names an agreement as its source; where the text says that a session chose or added something, that is an assistant's choice. Where an approved passage of a guide is concerned, what binds is what the passage says and not its wording: it can be reworded, shortened or moved like any other passage so long as nothing it says is lost. Sentences of sections 3 and 4 that draw a consequence for corrections from the user's words ("so", "therefore", "a correction should not") are this document's reading of those words. The consequences that bind are the ones the freeze document states as invariants. Section 4.12 lists the choices most easily mistaken for decisions.

An **observed fact** is something an investigator checked in a file, in the Git history or on disk. Sections 2, 4, 5, 7, 8 and 10 to 12 were written for the second run of the day, between commits `162b9e5e` and `aa4d34e1`, and their references to lines of the skill's files have been carried over mechanically, by the changed line ranges that Git reports, to commit `893392d5`, the last commit that changed the skill before this run. A reference to a line that a later commit itself changed keeps the old number, and then points near the place and not at it. Sections 1, 3, 6, 9 and 13 to 16 were written for this run on the evening of 25 November against `893392d5`; sections 17 and 18, and the passages of sections 3, 4.6, 4.13 and 13.2 that report the user's words of 26 November, were written on that day against `5a298b8e`, the commit of round 1; sections 19 and 20 were written against `bde1628a`, the tree that round 3 read. As rounds are applied the line numbers drift; they are places to start looking. The sections written for the second run still speak from its position where this did not need changing: in sections 4, 5, 7, 8 and 10 to 12, "this run" and "the present run" mean the second run of 25 November, "the morning's run" or "the morning's round" means the first, and "the other session" is the one that built the finalize pipeline. Where such a sentence says what the run's agents may open or must do, the freeze document of the third run decides.

A **recorded claim** is something written down earlier that was not checked against the present tree for this document: what a session wrote in a commit message, a memory note or a conversation, or what an investigator inferred without checking. It is evidence of what its author believed.

A **measurement** of sections 13 to 15 is a count or a size produced by a script, and the investigation report it comes from names the script. A classification that rests on an investigator's reading (this passage is padding, these two tests overlap) is that investigator's judgment. Neither has been judged by this run. A repairer who wishes to rely on one must first establish the fact in the actual file, and a judge must check it in the same way, as if it had come from nowhere. The actual file is, for a repairer, the copy of it that the round made, and for a judge the file itself; the two are equal unless another session committed during the round. The shortened versions of passages that the reports show as examples are evidence that a passage can be shortened; they are not approved text.

### Relation to the freeze document

The run is governed by its freeze document, `임시/do-math_검증_20261125-3/동결.md`, which fixes the files the run covers, the purpose and the invariants. This document supplies the context in which the freeze document is to be understood. If the two disagree, the freeze document governs. How a correction, a proposal for the user, or a report of a defect that shows in a file in scope and has its cause outside the skill (the contracts call it 발견) is written and handled is fixed by the contracts of the run, which each agent is given, and not by this document. For a merger, who combines overlapping proposals into one, the limits of sections 4 and 6 apply to the combined text as they apply to each proposal.

## 2. What do-math is

This section is a summary for orientation, drawn from `SKILL.md` and the guides as they stood on 25 November. It is not a standard against which a guide is judged.

do-math is a skill by which an AI agent, at a user's request, sets up and runs autonomous mathematical research on one proposition. The agent who talks with the user is called the **operator**. It fixes the proposition with the user, prepares a research **instance** (a directory with its own Git repository), and starts a detached program, the **runner**, which carries the research forward after the conversation ends. The proposition the instance exists to settle is its **root**.

The knowledge of an instance is its **canon**: one Lean 4 library, with a written **record** beside each declaration. A declaration together with its record is an **element**. A definition is an element; so is a statement, whether or not it yet has a proof. A proof is either complete in Lean, or it is written in words in the record while its Lean proof names the elements it rests on and ends in `sorry`. Review judges each proof twice, whether it is correct and whether a correct proof is accepted, and a statement accordingly has one of four **standings**: no proof; proof not confirmed; correct but held back; accepted. A statement is **settled** when one of its proofs is accepted and every statement that proof names is settled. An element is **formal** when its proof is complete in Lean and depends, through everything it uses, only on the allowed axioms (`propext`, `Classical.choice`, `Quot.sound`; never `sorryAx`). The **frontier** is the view computed from the root downward along what each proof names, showing every statement that is not settled. The instance is complete when the root, or its negation, is settled by a formal proof.

The runner launches fresh AI conversations called **sessions**, each in one of six roles. An **attempt** reads the frontier and chooses work that remains. An **explore** session holds a **question**, something the research wants to understand, and develops understanding of it; question `q0` is the root problem as a whole. A **formalizer** writes the Lean proof that a settled statement's written proof still lacks. A **peer** takes over the work of one of those three, continues it, and writes the **candidate**, the proposed change to the canon. A **review** session has three independent sub-reviewers examine the candidate, compiles it itself, and judges each element. An **organizer** joins what is the same by proving agreement in Lean, rearranges modules and merges questions. To avoid two sessions doing the same work, a session takes the **lease** of a statement and registers the **section** of it that it will work on, which is the whole statement or an exact part of what it asserts; an explorer takes a question in the same way. A registered section holds until the work on it has been published. Review judges and does not gate: useful imperfect work is published with review's judgments.

Each session runs under a **session host**, a process that stays alive across the session's turns and resumes the same conversation when a turn ends before the work is done. All Lean compilation in an instance goes through that instance's **Lean broker**, a service that schedules compiles within memory and thread budgets and keeps a cache of compiled modules. Three further commands work from a canon and are run by the operator, outside the runner. `depmap.py` draws the **dependency map**. `paper.py` (the **paper command**) has sessions write a mathematical paper from the canon and typesets it; its sessions are not research sessions, and a run of it is called here a paper run. `finalize.py` (the **finalize pipeline**, installed on 25 November) turns a finished research, one whose root has a formal proof, into a **package**: a folder `solved/<name>/` that holds the Lean library reduced to what the chosen proof needs, its records, the paper, the dependency map, a history of the research, an archive of its knowledge, a README and a licence, and that can be published as it is. The pipeline runs its own sessions in a fixed order of stages, each stage closed by a check the program makes (its exit check), and it calls the paper command for the paper. `package.py` holds the part of the pipeline's work that needs no session.

Some further terms recur in this document. A session is one conversation of an AI command-line client, either Claude Code (`claude`) or the Codex CLI (`codex`); the guides and notes call that client the CLI and a session's CLI process a worker. This document calls a session of one of the six roles a worker session or a research session; the two names mean the same. What a session is given to read at its start is its **pack**. The check that the runner makes of a finished session's output, of its form and its references and not of its mathematics, is the **transport check**. The **index** is the table the program extracts from the compiled library after each publication: for every declaration its kind, its statement, what it uses and the axioms it depends on. Besides its record, a statement carries its **approaches tried**, one entry for each line of attack pursued on it with where that line stopped, and a question carries its **understanding so far**. A record gives its element a **weight**, unit or step, by which the dependency map and the paper decide what to show as a result in its own right. A **concern** is a challenge that a session reports against an element's proof; a **finding** is a judgment of review that withdraws a proof's confirmation (the paper command and the finalize pipeline use the same word for something else, a single thing that a session reading a manuscript or a package reports; the two have nothing to do with each other); a **comprehensive review** is an assignment of the review role to examine questioned proofs and their uses. A statement whose negation is settled is **refuted**, and a statement and its negation that are both settled are **contested**. A **standing input** is a text that the operator submits with the `input` command and that every session launched afterwards receives. It is how the user's conditions reach a running instance, and the notes also call it a standing condition. The roles that produce work for a peer to take over (attempt, explore, formalizer) are the producing roles; when a candidate cannot be published, the runner assigns a new peer to the same work, a replacement peer. The **literature server** is scholarly-mcp, a server through which sessions search for and fetch mathematical literature; settings and status output call it `scholarly`.

Two research instances are named throughout. The **parking** instance (주차함수: that a certain count attached to parking functions is unimodal) belonged to the third generation, which also required a formal Lean proof of the root, kept in Lean files beside its prose records. It ran from 19 to 23 November, proved its root formally, and was the instance through which the user gave most of the conditions that the guides now carry. Its proof was afterwards put into the form of a fourth-generation canon with `init`. An instance made in that way has a canon and no sessions; this document calls it a canon-only instance. The paper and map commands have been run on canon-only instances of the parking proof and of a second proof of the same result, which the notes call the staircase proof. The **sunflower** instance (해바라기: the Erdős–Rado sunflower conjecture) is the first instance of the fourth generation to import Mathlib. It has run since 22 November and is running now (section 6).

The skill consists of an entry point for the operator (`SKILL.md`), reference documents (`reference/`), one guide per role (`roles/`), short texts shown to a session when it is resumed (`resume/`), the instructions given to the sessions of the finalize pipeline (`finalize/`), the program (`runtime/`) with its tests (`runtime/tests/`), and a collection of real mathematical papers used as models by the paper command (`exemplars/`). Section 7 maps these in detail.

The skill has been rebuilt several times, and the notes speak of **generations**. In the third generation, which ran until 21 November, knowledge was kept as prose records, "nodes" for exact statements, "relations" between them and a glossary. The **fourth generation**, committed on 22 November as `ceb38646`, replaced all of that with the Lean library described above. The fourth generation is what this run covers. The directory `.agents/skills/do-math/` is a copy of the skill, generated by a script from `.claude/skills/do-math/` for another AI runtime; the notes call it the **mirror** and call the original the **canonical tree**.

## 3. What the user asked this run to do

The request was first made on 25 November 2026 at 03:35 KST and given again, in the same words, at 11:43 KST. At 21:37 KST, after the two runs that answered it had been closed, the user gave it a third time with four sentences added. Its part about the skill now reads, in full:

> "@.claude/skills/do-math\ has gone through a lot of changes in the past week or so. I want to fix the issues that may have been introduced. Things that I want fixed include, reducing doc bloat, reducing test bloat, improving test coverage, increasing test efficiency, fixing script bugs, updating outdated links and contents, improving coherence and consistency, and more. And this "more" is really important, because those things that I just laid out are open lists. [...] Really put a focus on reducing bloat in docs and tests, simplifying out overengineering in code(without losing actual needed value or causing issues), patching up to improve robustness where lacking, etc etc. Most important goal should be reducing bloat in docs, in a way that does not sacrifice its intricate details and value, with freedom in selection of means to achieve it. fixers are encouraged to diagnose and propose solutions to big, structural problems that span multiple areas if existing, as well as detailed fixes"

The omitted part asks for the investigation that produced this document, for a run of at most sixteen rounds, and adds: "do not run tests more than once every 4 rounds, and only when needed."

The first part names seven kinds of defect and says twice that the list is open. The added sentences do three things. They rank: the documents first, then the tests, the program's excess machinery and its weak places. They bound and free the work on the documents at once: no detail and no value is to be lost, and the means are the repairer's to choose. And they ask for more than local corrections: where a problem comes from how material is divided and arranged across several files, the user wants it diagnosed and a solution proposed.

Why the user added them can be read from what the two earlier runs did. They applied 255 corrections. In the same hours the guides grew from 739 KB to 999 KB as Git holds them, of which 208 KB came with the installation of a new command (its sixteen instruction files, and what the installation wrote into two existing guides) and the rest with corrections and other changes; three weeks earlier, on 5 November, the guides held 193 KB (section 5.6). The freeze document of the second run forbade a correction to reduce the content of a guide unless it proved where the reader still receives what was removed, told repairers to leave a passage as it was wherever it was unclear whether it was padding, and gave no repairer the task of looking for bulk. This run changes all three: bulk is the first thing every repairer of a guide looks for, a passage that might be padding is analysed and not passed over, and a correction that makes a guide longer has to show that it could not be done by rewording.

### What "doc bloat" means here

The user has spoken about the bulk of the guides five times, the last of them while this run was under way, and the statements together fix what this run may and may not do.

On 21 November, after a session had added several passages to the guides, the user asked for a tidying and said what kind (session `1f8bb240`, 16:00:21Z):

> "전반적으로 내용에서 지금의 스타일과 전달하는 내용 및 디테일들은 유지하면서도 중복이라던지 과하게 장황한 것 등은 정리해보고 싶어. 그러니까, 내용을 압축하는 것이 아니라 내용을 유지하면서 그에 더해 불필요한 군더더기를 덜어내는"

(Keep the present style, the content conveyed and the details, and tidy away duplication and excessive verbosity; that is, do not compress the content, but keep the content and remove the unnecessary padding on top of it.) The session then removed three kinds of text: an explanation restated in one document when its main statement stood in another document that the same reader receives, repetition within one document, and connecting sentences that carried no content (memory note `M-0734`, correction 5). It applied this only to the passages it had itself written; the rest of the guides were never tidied in this way.

On 22 November, starting a revision of the guides, the user set the aim in one sentence (session `20ae7c1d`, 05:36:20Z): "Let's focus on improving the guidance in both effectiveness, efficiency, while retaining delicate and accurate details".

On 24 November an audit of the whole skill for over-engineering reported to the user, among its largest findings, this one, with the simpler design it proposed (session `20538f79`, 13:35Z):

> "**Guides every session carries and does not use** | Each session's starting pack is 430 to 730 KB. One guide of 50 KB goes to all roles and its main section was used by one session in 267. Twelve rules are stated in two to five documents each. | Each rule once, in the guide of the role that acts on it."

The user answered that item with one word, "intended" (13:46:18Z). The memory note of that day records it among the things the user kept (`M-0772`).

On 25 November the user asked this run to make the reduction of bulk in the documents its most important goal, "in a way that does not sacrifice its intricate details and value, with freedom in selection of means to achieve it".

On 26 November, after round 1 of this run, the driving session reported that the round had shortened the guides by 1.7 percent and laid out what it had been keeping because of the answer "intended": every statement of a rule in every document where it stands. The user answered that they were "not sure if 'intended' was said to apply to those", and then said how repetition is to be handled (session `4e9d0f64`, 15:55:10Z on 25 November, which is 00:55 KST on the 26th):

> "about rules restated, rather than approaching it as 'something is triggered n times where then m times there', really read and understand what is written where how and why, and couple that with the understanding over roles, designs, structures and intentions what each docs should contain as well as what each pack should contain through its composition, and that should be how repetition is restructured."

In the same exchange the user decided four further things. The procedure by which a peer writes the account of `q0` comes out of the peer guide: "yeah taking out q0 account procedure from peer.md into a separate thing would be better." (15:46:47Z), and with it the standard to which that account is written: "yeah move the standard into the same document too" (16:40:37Z). Two kinds of knowledge need not be told to research sessions: "I think sessions don't really need to know about the system-level lifecycle knowledge." (15:46:47Z) and "broker scheduling and cache compile knowledge is not really important for sessions to know." (15:55:10Z). And `reference/evidence.md`, a guide that no pack carries, is to give what it alone says to `reference/common.md` and then to go: "yeah do the evidence.md plan" (16:11:22Z), in answer to the driving session's comparison of that document, paragraph by paragraph, with what the packs already hold. A fifth followed when the driving session reported that the section "Writing the texts of the canon" of `reference/writing.md` is written for the peer and goes to every role: "I want it moved" (16:51:40Z). Sections 16 to 18 carry these out.

Later on 26 November, while rounds 3 and 4 were under way, the user decided further matters that the driving session had put with two design reports (sections 19 and 20) and with two proposals of round 4. On the operator's documents: "Q1 yes, Q2 yes, Q8 no, Q6 no, shared standard no" (02:10:54Z). That is, two accounts leave `reference/operations.md` (how the page of the dependency map behaves and how the map is drawn, and what each session of the paper command is given); the passage on what a submission to the registry still needs stays; `operations.md` is not divided into several files; and a writing session and its checking session of the pipeline are not given one shared file for a standard that both are told. Asked then whether `operations.md` should be merged into `SKILL.md`, and told that `SKILL.md` is loaded whole whenever the skill is invoked while `operations.md` is opened by section, the user said: "ok keep them separate" (02:18:12Z). On the part of `reference/lean.md` that speaks of memory: "the goal of that part is to direct the sessions toward behaviors that save memory usage effectively" (03:09:44Z). And in the same message, on a session of the paper command or of the pipeline that a provider refuses for lack of capacity or of credit: "wait and relaunch". Sections 16.8, 18, 19 and 20 carry these out.

The reading this run adopts is the following, and the freeze document makes it the rule of the run. It is the driving session's reading of the four statements together, and not a further statement of the user's.

*What is kept.* The detail of the guides. The freeze document calls it 세부 and defines it as whatever a passage gives its reader that the reader can use in the reader's own work: to judge, to act, to check, or to decide a case the text does not name. Conditions, exceptions, steps of an argument, criteria of judgment, worked cases, numbers, names, commands and paths, an account of how something works, the reason for a rule, and the concrete wording that came from the user's own conditions are detail. Section 4 and the guides themselves say where wording is the user's (a quotation, "the user asked"); other wording can be changed. The style of the guides is kept too, which gives a session knowledge to judge by and not commands (section 4.3). Which guides each role receives, which the user called intended on 24 November, is kept, with the one exception the user made on 26 November for the account of `q0` (section 16). A correction does not otherwise change the composition of a pack.

*What is removed.* Text that can go, or be said in less, with every detail kept. The freeze document calls it 군더더기 and lists the forms the investigation found: sentences and paragraphs that say in many words what fewer would say, among them a sentence that restates the one before and a qualifier added by one correction that folds into the sentence it qualifies; the same fact told again in other words to a reader who has it already, within one document or in documents received together; a pointer that lists what the place it points to contains; sentences that announce or sum up; the history of how the text or the program came to be, and text about what no longer exists; facts of one form written out as prose where a table or a list would hold them. For a matter that is told in several documents of one pack, the user's instruction of 26 November replaces the rule that rounds up to round 1 followed, which kept every statement of a rule wherever it stood. Whether a telling stays, and in what form, is decided from an understanding of what each place says and why, of what each role does, of what each document is for and of what each pack gives its reader. Section 17 holds that understanding and, for each such matter, where its full telling is to live and what the other places keep.

*What is neither.* Two kinds of text need a closer rule, and the freeze document gives it. The measurements of one machine and the account of one incident are specific, but the user asked that the guides say what holds everywhere (section 4.3). Such a passage can be reduced to the reason it supports and to the size a reader needs for an estimate, unless the reader judges by the number itself. And an account of how a program works inside, told to a reader who only calls it, is detail that stands in the wrong place. It is not deleted on the ground that this reader does not use it. It stays once where the same reader can find it, and a second telling points there. Taking it out of a pack altogether changes what a role receives and is put to the user. For two kinds of such knowledge the user has decided: how the system manages the life of a session, and how the broker schedules compiles and uses its cache, leave the guides of research sessions and stay with the operator (section 18).

*How.* By any means that keeps the detail: rewording, folding sentences and paragraphs together, reordering a section, tables and lists for facts of one form, moving a passage to the place where its reader needs it, merging and splitting documents. This is the difference between removing padding and compressing, which the user ruled out on 21 November. Compressing replaces particular statements by a general one, and the particulars are gone. Removing padding leaves every particular statement in place and takes away the words that carried none.

*Who the reader is.* Whether two places repeat each other depends on who receives both, and when. A worker session receives the guides of its role's pack at one time and a resume text hours later; a session of the finalize pipeline receives `finalize/common.md` and one instruction file; a session of the paper command receives the method document, or one section of it, and a prompt; the operator reads `SKILL.md` and `reference/operations.md` (section 7.2). The same content in the texts of two different readers is not repetition. A resume text that says again what a guide of the pack says is not padding for that reason alone, and neither is a passage that sessions go back to after their context has been compacted, when the rest of the pack is gone (section 13.1). The freeze document's invariant on detail says, for each kind of guide, where a detail must still stand for its reader to have it.

Section 13 says where the bulk was found and in which of these forms.

### What the user asks of tests

The user's requirements for the tests of this skill were given for an earlier verification run and recorded in English in its freeze document (`임시/do-math_검증_20261105/동결.md:56`):

> "(d) tests of the actual intended runtime behavior rather than a divergent imitation, assertions capable of failing when behavior deviates, and simple, cost-effective tests without unnecessary machinery; (e) complete coverage within each test module and across the suite, without needless overlap, redundancy or combined complexity."

The three test-related goals of the request are these requirements seen from the side of their failure: tests that overlap or carry machinery they do not need ("test bloat"), behaviour that no test would notice breaking ("test coverage"), and tests that cost more time than what they check requires ("test efficiency"). The suite is larger than the program it tests, 1,581 KB against 1,207 KB, and one sequential run of its twenty-three modules took forty-three minutes on this machine on the afternoon of 25 November (section 8). The user asked on 22 November "why do the tests take so long" (session `3750b5d4`, 19:20:27Z) and nothing was changed in response. Section 14 gives what was measured, and section 11 the repository's standing criteria for judging a test.

These goals can pull against one another, and one way of trading them is closed: merging two tests into one so that an assertion which only one of them made is lost. The judges of an earlier run rejected such a proposal, and the freeze document makes it a rule. Reducing a test's cost has to preserve the starting state and the real path it exercises, and not only its assertions.

### What the user asks of the program

"Simplifying out overengineering in code (without losing actual needed value or causing issues), patching up to improve robustness where lacking." Section 4.7 gives what the user has said this week about repairs, and the two requests are to be read with it. Simplifying means removing what nothing uses, joining what is implemented twice, and taking out machinery built for a case that does not occur; the user's words for it on 24 November were "delete what is not used" and "Simplicity is best". The limits are the mechanisms the user decided to keep (section 4.6) and everything a running instance depends on (section 6). Robustness means that the program holds under the conditions real operation brings: a file another process has open, a child process that dies, a provider that refuses, malformed input arriving from outside. It does not mean a recovery path for a state that only the program's own defect could produce; the user refused exactly that on 24 November ("building a failsafe for that exception is the wrong path to take"; section 4.7). Section 15 gives what was measured.

### What the user asks about running tests and other checks

During this week the user stated a principle about checks, after watching sessions repeat them (session `20538f79`, 24 November 12:13:09Z):

> "a lot of the procedures are inefficient and repetitive. for example, subagent running the tests before reporting, then the receiving agent running the same ... tests all over again, running expensive builds only to run builds again like seconds later, running entire-scope verification while changing pieces of it which incur running entire-scope verification again afterwards, running whole tests after changing some doc that PHYSICALLY CANNOT CHANGE TEST RESULTS, a lot of the procedures done ad-hoc are more like a facade of work"

The session's own summary of the principle, which the user let stand, was: "A check is worth running only when its result is not already settled. That means something it depends on has changed since it was last run on the same bytes." On the evening of 25 November, while the driving session was confirming changes made after the second run, the user asked whether tests that had already run were being run again and had a running module stopped. The limit on test runs in the request follows from this principle, and from the fact that the machine is also carrying live research (section 6). In this run the repairers, the mergers and the judges do not run the tests. The driving session runs tests at most once in each window of four rounds (rounds 1 to 4, 5 to 8, 9 to 12, 13 to 16), after the last round of the window or of the run, and then only the modules whose result is not settled: the modules that can be affected by a file that has changed since they last ran. A change to a guide alone can count, because some tests assert sentences of the guides. Between those runs a correction to the program or to a test is accepted on reading alone, which is why a correction that changes behaviour has to carry the change to the test that asserts that behaviour, why a correction that removes or merges tests has to name where each assertion remains, and why a judge who cannot follow a change to its end by reading rejects it.

### What the user said of the design on 26 November, and the evaluation it was said of

On 26 November, while this run was in its fourth and fifth rounds, the user had another session evaluate the quality and value of the skill's folder (section 21). When the user had read the evaluation, the user said of it (session `14ebe8ba`, 03:47:46Z):

> "most of your findings are a result of a deliberate design choice, where the system is designed to rely mostly on the good behavior of models and their instruction following capability, rather than setting restrictions and blocks to go around, for simplicity of system"

Three minutes later the user had the findings handed to this run (03:51:02Z): "deliver your findings to the verify session so that it can have its workers evaluate them for proper addressing".

The first statement says of the system as a whole what the user had said on 22 November of the limits placed on sessions (section 4.2). The freeze document carries it under 「결함이 아닌 것」: what can happen only if a session or the operator acts otherwise than instructed is not a defect, and no correction adds a check or a restriction against it. Two things lie outside the statement as the driving session reads it, and the freeze document names them. One is program logic that goes wrong although every session does what it is told. The other is a defect of the instructions themselves: a guide that leaves undecided a case a session meets, or two passages that tell a session different things. Such a defect is closed by correcting the text, and not by adding a check. This reading is the driving session's, and not a further statement of the user's.

The second statement is why section 21 exists and why, from round 7, each repairer is given the findings that concern its files.

### What the user said on 26 November about which documents the reduction of bulk concerns first

While round 7 was under way the driving session laid out the matters that waited for the user, and the user answered in one line (26 November, 05:43:38Z):

> "C', give those sessions full. the main doc that I care about is the runner sessions, the rest can keep some more if serving function, remove"

The line answers four things. "C'" chooses among the options on which occurrences count as the account name in a package (section 16.9). "give those sessions full" returns the method document, as a whole, to the sessions of the paper command that compare a section with its elements (section 16.10). "remove" had two folders that a test run had left in the system's temporary folder deleted, which was done.

The third part bears on the whole run: "the main doc that I care about is the runner sessions, the rest can keep some more if serving function". The documents whose bulk the user most wants reduced are the guides that the sessions launched by the runner receive, the six roles of the research. The other guides, the operator's documents and the instructions the two commands give their sessions, may keep more where the content serves its reader. The freeze document draws the consequence, and the consequence is the driving session's reading: in those other documents the repair of defects comes before the reduction of bulk, and a passage that serves its reader is not reworded to make it shorter; what serves no reader there (a second copy the same reader already has, text about what no longer exists) still goes.

### What the user decided on 26 November on three proposals of rounds 7 and 8

Rounds 7 and 8 put three matters to the user, and the user answered them in three messages while the tests of round 8 ran (26 November, 07:56:30Z, 07:57:43Z and 08:15:39Z).

On what an attempt does when every statement of the frontier is held by other sessions, the user first asked "doesn't attempt already end with no research in that case?", was told that `roles/attempt.md` says so and that one sentence of `reference/common.md` can be read otherwise, and then decided: "well, if an attempt can find something new to work on outside frontier, good on them." An attempt may take on work outside the frontier in that case as well (section 16.13).

On findings about citations when the canon has no bibliography, a matter section 4.13 lists as unanswered, the user first asked "how about just adding the missing citation?". The driving session answered that a writing session may not add a work nobody read, that the pipeline's sources step already finds sources of results known under a name, and that finding the earlier work on the problem itself would be a new capability. The user then decided: "let's focus on ensuring that missing citation will not occur later down the line in downward tasms". The driving session reads this as follows and told the user so: the finding of the reading is not silenced, and no step that hunts for citations is added to the paper stage; what is to be secured is that the research already holds the literature its paper needs when the paper or the package is written (section 16.14).

On the records of declarations that the pipeline does not change, which a package carries as the research wrote them, the user decided to leave this as it is ("for 2, 1"; section 16, among the proposals not adopted).

## 4. What the user has decided

This section lists what the user has laid down about how do-math works and how its documents are written, so far as the investigation found it. A correction that reverses or narrows one of these decisions is not a repair. Where a correction would need such a change, the matter is for the user.

The user's decisions for the fourth generation were first collected in an agreement of twenty-five decisions and a set of approved proposals, written in Korean before the rebuild: `임시/do-math-lean-설계/합의.md`. The design record `docs/do-math/lean-canon-design.md` restates them in English with their reasons. Both are among the sources of this run. Two things about them need to be known. First, the design record was deliberately not updated after 22 November; later decisions were written into sections of `docs/do-math/lean-canon-verification.md` and into memory notes, and the sessions adopted the convention that where the design record and the present guides and code differ, the guides and code are current. That convention is an assistant's choice, but the later decisions it serves are the user's and are listed below. Second, the agreement is an assistant's wording of what the user wanted, approved as a whole with the single word "승인" (approved). It binds. But the wording of an approved text has more than once been read more widely than the user meant, so wherever this section can give the user's own words it gives those.

An older source also still applies in part: a list of forty criteria for do-math, derived from the user's statements and approved by the user on 7 November, at `임시/do-math_검증_20261107/사용자기준.md`. Its criteria about mathematical rigor and evidence (group E) were written for the nodes, relations, glossary and record forms of the earlier generations, and were superseded where they name those objects. Its criteria about the purpose and identity of the system (A), a light runner and the sessions' own judgment (B), guides as knowledge and not rules (C), the attitude of research (D), the quality of documents and prose (F), fidelity to the scope of the user's intent (G), the substance of tests (H) and operation and running instances (I) still stand unless a later decision of the user changed the thing they speak of, and several are cited below by their letter and number. The same file lists directions the user rejected (R-1 and following).

Where a guide and the program disagree, neither is the standard by itself. The standard is what the user decided; where that is silent, the agreement and the design record so far as no later decision of the user changed them; and where these too are silent, the repairer judges from the purpose of the mechanism which side is wrong, and says why. Where a guide and the program agree with each other but both depart from the agreement or the design record, and no later decision of the user that accounts for the difference is found in this document, in the verification record, or in the message and the memory note of the commit that brought the behaviour in, the matter is put to the user and not turned back by a correction: a decision may have been made that the records do not show.

### 4.1 What the fourth generation is for

The user's brief for the rebuild (session `d778f997`, 21 November 15:14:03Z; the same text in session `e13e3c64`) asked for five things: that the problem and every proposition and relation around it be expressed in Lean and laid out, not only a few key statements; that the record of mathematical content, one per Lean element and native to Lean's structure, be separated from the record of process; that sessions be able to traverse the canon mechanically and deterministically, and be told to, so that repeated work drops; that a script calling AI sessions produce a paper and its PDF from the canon; and that the use of Lean be fused into the whole of the work, "다만 자원 소비가 효율적인 방식으로" (but in a way that consumes resources efficiently). The brief ends with a principle that governs how old and new are to be weighed:

> "만약 지금 기존의 절차와 이 새로운 제안이 서로 충돌한다 느낀다면 애시당초 기존의 절차를 개선하는 것이 목적이기 때문이야. 그러니 어떻게 하면 기존의 절차를 이 새로운 제안에도 불구하고 유지할 수 있을까, 는 애시당초 방향이 틀린 고민인거지."

(If the existing procedure and this proposal seem to conflict, that is because improving the existing procedure is the purpose; asking how to keep the old procedure in spite of the proposal is the wrong question from the start.)

Two requirements from the design conversation bear directly on bloat inside an instance. The user wanted records not to keep accumulating, "런타임이 길어져도 bloat이 최소화되도록" (so that bloat stays minimal even as the run grows long; 16:30:52Z), and wanted what sessions learn to accumulate by the object it concerns and not one record per session (16:34:29Z). And on unformalized results the user set a direction (17:37:23Z): the rule is not "discard what is not expressed in Lean" but "express it in Lean and keep it", and expressing things in Lean is to be a central step of the work from its start, "자연어 처리의 위에 더해지는 chore가 아니라" (not a chore added on top of work done in natural language).

The agreement, and the design record after it, list what the fourth generation left as it was, and the user approved that list with the rest: the six roles and the order in which the runner launches them; the way questions are proposed and admitted; concerns, findings and the comprehensive review; the two acceptance settings (section 4.5); that a session's lease and section hold until its work is published; the reconsideration of a session's ending, by which a session that ends is resumed to judge again whether it should; that the original material of a session is kept, and the limit on who reads it; English inside an instance; and the governing principles of the earlier design record `docs/do-math/rebuild-design.md`, which remains in force for whatever the new record does not change.

### 4.2 The sessions judge; the machine does not

The oldest and most often repeated of the user's requirements is that judgments of mathematical meaning belong to the sessions and that the program records and carries out what they decide (criteria B1 to B4). The program refuses only what a machine can decide: that Lean confirms a statement is the same as another or its negation, that something compiles, which axioms a proof depends on, names and hashes, the form of files, who holds a lease. A correction that makes the program judge mathematical content, the faithfulness of a statement, the sufficiency of an argument or the meaning of an overlap between sections goes against this.

There is no planning role and no assignment of work: exactly six roles, and attempts choose their own work (criterion A2).

The limits placed on sessions rest on their cooperation, and this is deliberate. When a comparison of the skill against outside standards reported that sessions can write to every file the checks rely on, the user answered (session `20ae7c1d`, 22 November 05:36:20Z): "restrictions are intentionally reliant on session's cooperation for effectiveness, efficiency, and simplicity." The absence of an enforcing mechanism is therefore not a defect, and a correction should not add enforcing wording or machinery. On 26 November, on reading an evaluation of the skill, the user said the same of the system as a whole (sections 3 and 21): it is "designed to rely mostly on the good behavior of models and their instruction following capability, rather than setting restrictions and blocks to go around, for simplicity of system".

The same holds for the operator. During a verification run on 10 November the user gave this instruction, which later freeze documents have carried forward (original in `임시/do-math_검증_20261110-2/동결.md:76-80`):

> "there is no need to consider a case of intentionally destructive and hostile operator or issues that would arise only in such a circumstance. on the contrary, if such considerations lead to rule-ification of what should be sessions' semantic judgement, restrict valuable degree of freedom, or overengineer the system into unnecessary complexity, or create rules that does not improve the effectiveness of research and/or decrease its effectiveness, such considerations are to be actively addressed"

So a problem that arises only when the operator deliberately breaks the system is not a defect, and code, text or tests that exist only to guard against such an operator, and that cost freedom or simplicity, are themselves defects.

On orchestration the user said (session `3171182f`, 23 November 00:34:54Z) that an orchestrating agent either knows the details of every area it delegates, in which case it need not delegate, or does not, in which case it gives directives that constrain the worker who meets the actual detail; "So, it is better to just set governance rules and tools that help agents orchestrate themselves, in my opinion." A correction that adds central direction of the sessions' work runs against this.

When sessions evaluated do-math this week the user corrected them more than once for judging by proxies and for treating deliberate positions as failures: evaluate "without mistaking any of its key qualities as failures or evaluating trade-offs as mistakes" (session `5172428b`, 22 November 14:18:33Z), and "Don't rely on proxies as sole basis of your evaluation; actually read what is there, what is implemented, and judge based on what actually is" (15:35:11Z). This document takes the same standard to apply to what a repairer calls a defect.

### 4.3 How the guides are written

**Knowledge for judgment, not rules.** The guides that worker sessions read (`roles/`, `resume/`, and the documents of `reference/` in their packs) are meant to give a session the understanding from which it judges, not conditions and commands (criteria C1 to C3, F1 to F5, G2). The operator's documents (`SKILL.md`, `reference/operations.md`) describe procedures and commands and are not under this limit. Nor are the method document of the paper command and the instruction files of the finalize pipeline: the first is the fixed way that the user asked a script to supply (section 4.10), and the second are the instructions of a program that holds the checks itself (section 4.11). A correction does not add if-then rules, lists of things to refuse, closed lists of considerations, mandatory fields for justification, or gates. A choice left to the session is written as reasons, costs and open examples. This limit does not apply to the contracts that a machine or another role consumes: the form of an output file, the arguments of a command.

On 21 November the user decided that the way of working and review's criteria for accepting a proof should nonetheless be written down explicitly, and in what manner (session `1f8bb240`, 14:37:22Z):

> "명시적 규범으로 적자, 다만 판단의 여지 없이 hard하게 적용하는 것이 아니라 soft하게 그를 guide하는 방식으로"

(Write them as explicit norms, but not applied hard with no room for judgment; in a way that softly guides it.) They are to be phrased directly: "the user asks ... 가 아니라 you should ... 식으로" (not "the user asks ..." but "you should ..."; 15:49:50Z). The essay `reference/what-research-is-for.md` was to be left as written, and its relation to the new norms explained in a paragraph of `reference/common.md` (14:44:01Z). That essay and `reference/user-writing-preferences.md` carry the user's own account of what research is for and of how the user wants things written. Earlier verification runs treated both as the user's texts and left them unchanged, and they are not among the files this run covers.

**English, with no reason given.** Everything inside an instance is written in English: "그냥 다 영어로 하자 인스턴스 내부 모든걸" (14:17:33Z). The user gave no reason and the guides state none; a correction should not supply one. The user's words reach an instance only as an English translation, and the operator keeps the original outside it (the same session, 14:30:50Z).

**What the user's conditions say, and nothing added.** In the parking instance, several things that review sessions and other sessions did wrong came from sentences the operator had added around the user's words: a reason the user had not given, a broader reading than the user meant. The operator guidance now tells the operator to convey what the user said and nothing more, and the same care applies to the guides themselves. In particular the reason for holding back a correct proof that can still be made simpler is that the simpler proof is more concise, and nothing else; the user rejected the reasons the operator had invented (cost of formalization, number of elements named). Section 4.5 gives the words.

**Definitions before use, and no private terms.** While the norms were being written the user had the session check its English against a rule that has since entered the repository's instructions: a sentence of the form "X is Y" burdens the reader when Y is a term the writer coined or one that needs context, so such a term is either avoided or defined before it is used (15:17:17Z). The session unpacked words it had been using as names, such as "room", "held back", "retreat", "indicator", "ledger".

**Generally applicable, not fitted to this machine.** When a session wrote into a guide where the imported library's sources lie on this computer, the user had it rewritten (session `54d4d2f6`, 22 November 20:05:47Z): "rather than saying something that is tailored to this local environment, say something more generally applicable like, check input.json or whatever". Guides state what follows from the runner's contract with a session, not the layout of the machine on which they were written.

**A question may or may not have the form of a statement.** The guides once defined a question as something "that has no form as a statement". The user corrected the reading (session `0860eb60`, 23 November 00:17:50Z): the phrase "was intended to be "inclusive" to even those without a statement, not to be "exclusive" only to those". The three definitions were reworded, and one sentence was added with the user's approval: "A question can also be about a statement, such as whether a conjecture left as a lead holds." The commit that made this change (`80625feb`) also added two paragraphs to `reference/common.md` on where sessions meet the canon, at the user's request ("do that passage explaining which places sessions are led to", 00:23:49Z). That the passage describes and does not prescribe is the session's choice, made in keeping with the first paragraph of this section.

**"No" is an answer.** Looking at explorations that kept working without closing their questions, the user said (session `ce7d1dca`, 22 November 06:03:00Z): "그러면 각 탐구들은 질문에 답한거 아닌가? No 도 답이잖아" (Then didn't each exploration answer its question? "No" is an answer too). The criterion for resolving a question was rewritten across the guides with the user's approval (06:46:53Z), including a paragraph giving the case. The user also preferred input that helps explorers work better in general over input that says "do this specifically" (06:15:55Z).

**Research conduct the user asked the guides to convey.** Sessions are to search actively for prior work and cite it "모든 것을 직접 재발명하는 대신" (instead of reinventing everything themselves; session `1f8bb240`, 15:02:47Z); an attempt is strongly encouraged to search and judges what it finds. The literature server is attached to every instance by default, opened only to sources that need no login (the user's choice among three options, 15:25:06Z; made a default of the code on 22 November). A finite extension of the range over which something has been checked is not in itself an achievement; closing what remained, or gaining structure, pattern, insight or a lead while extending, is, and the examples are an open list whose common feature is that they change what is known (decision of 10 November, memory note `M-0687`; a correction that turns the examples into a list of conditions narrows the principle).

### 4.4 What counts as proved

Three things are fixed by the agreement and by criterion A3. The default allowed axioms are `propext`, `Classical.choice` and `Quot.sound`, and `sorryAx` is never allowed. A complete Lean proof brought by a session is confirmed only after review has compiled the candidate itself. And the standard for the instance's completion, a formal proof of the root or of its negation, is not to be lowered.

This week the question of what must be trusted beyond Lean was decided. A second check of formal proofs outside the program's own index, here called the formal judge, had been added on 22 November with the user's approval: the compiled declarations were exported and compared by an external program, `lake comparator`. On 24 November, after it had stopped a live runner, the user asked what it was for and then decided (session `20538f79`, 12:42:15Z and 12:46:54Z):

> "is that really necessary? if statement is true and if lean is compiled then it is proved, as long as we do it right. It feels wasteful to create this elaborate setup to prepare for a case where it is not done right, instead of just doing it right"
>
> "I feel we are very much overcomplicating a very much simple issue, on the issue of lean's reliability"

The formal judge was removed, and the program now takes the axioms a declaration depends on from Lean's own report. A correction should not reintroduce a formal judge in any form: an export of the compiled declarations, a second program that checks them again, or a comparison by an external comparator. The program still compares the root's statement and the definitions under it with the instance's first commit when the root becomes formal (the "root check"). That check sees less than the formal judge did: it does not see a private helper of a definition, nor a definition whose unchanged text Lean now reads as a different term. The user was told and has not ruled on it; it is a known limit, not a defect to repair by rebuilding what was removed.

Review compiles a candidate itself without the cache although the peer has compiled it already. The over-engineering audit listed this as duplication; the user kept it: "review recompile is planned redundancy, it ensures that they compile" (13:46:18Z).

The user considered replacing the Lean broker's batch compiles with a live Lean server and decided against it: "I think it would be better to just keep the broker as is then" (session `ecf4e723`, 22 November 17:26:40Z).

### 4.5 When review accepts a correct proof

Review asks two things of a proof it has found correct before accepting it: whether the proof can still clearly be made simpler, and whether it closes part of the statement by large computation done without understanding. The guides call these the two considerations; the settings call them the acceptance settings (`acceptance.decline_reducible_proofs` and `acceptance.decline_uncomprehended_computation`, both true by default); section 4.3 called them review's criteria for accepting a proof. The user decided two separate things about them (session `1f8bb240`, 21 November 14:34:36Z and 14:42:44Z). They apply in every instance unless a setting turns one off, which is what the user's "기본값은 강하게 적용" (the default applies strongly) referred to, as the user explained when the session had read it otherwise (14:42:44Z: "기본값이 강하게 적용이라 말했을 때는 범위를 말한 것이었고", when I said the default applies strongly I was speaking of the scope). And their content is guidance for review's judgment, with no thresholds.

These considerations began as conditions the user gave to the parking instance, and the user corrected the operator's rendering of them three times. The corrections fix their meaning (session `58a24e38`):

- The reason for the first (21 November 03:14:03Z): "선언된 의존이 형식화 비용이라서 줄이라 한게 아니야. 그냥 줄일 수 있으면 그게 더 간결하니까 줄이라 한거지." (I did not say to reduce because declared dependencies cost formalization effort. I said to reduce because, if it can be reduced, that is more concise.)
- On computation (the same day, 03:14:03Z and 03:32:48Z): the problem is not that a proof relies on computed results; "내가 바라는 것은 계산 결과로 당장 그것이 참이란 것을 보일 수 있다 해도 가능하면 왜 참인지를 설명해주는 보다 수학적인 연구를 계속 수행하란 것" (what I want is that, even if computation can show at once that it is true, the more mathematical research that explains why it is true continues where possible). And: "내가 말한건, 무식하게 이해 없이 큰 단위 계산을 하는 것이었고 모든 계산을 금지한게 아니야" (what I meant was large-scale computation done crudely, without understanding; I did not forbid all computation). A case the user judged not to be large-scale: reading a few dozen facts and a few thousand entries from a table already published (21 November 09:01:48Z, "이 정도는 큰 단위 계산이 아니라 생각해").
- On citing part of a published result (03:04:42Z): "일부라고 해도 써야 하면 안 쓸 이유는 없잖아?" (even if only part of it is used, if it is needed there is no reason not to use it).
- The same criterion applies to a proof that derives one statement from another (20 November 23:18:25Z).
- Every session is to judge by reading the actual texts, "단순 피상적인 지표들을 계산해 그를 통해 프록시로 판단을 내리는게 아니라" (not by computing superficial indicators and judging through them as proxies; 20 November 16:35:13Z).

One request from the same session reached the guides only on 25 November. When a review session rejected a correct result because of one wrong side sentence, the user asked that review be able to support the result and leave a note, added this as a condition of the run, and said (22 November 04:26:56Z): "상시 조건으로 넣자, 그리고 검토자 지침에도 반영해줘 정본에" (put it in as a standing condition, and also reflect it in the guidance for review, in the canonical tree). The scope the user finally set was narrow (04:34:38Z): "지금 문제가 되었던 것 하나만. 사소하고 검토자가 어떤 점에서 왜 다르게 보아야 하는지를 의견으로 남기는 것 만으로 차후 그 지지된 결과를 읽을 이들이 올바르게 문제 없이 결과를 받아 정확하게 사용할 수 있는 경우 vs 아닌 경우" (only the one thing that was the problem now: the case where the flaw is minor, and where the reviewer's leaving a note of what is to be read differently and why is enough for those who later read the result to take it correctly and use it accurately, as against the case where that is not so). In the third generation a result that review found sound was "supported"; the request is that review, in the first case, not reject the result for the flaw. The session that received the request added only a standing input to the instance then running. The verification round of the morning of 25 November carried it into `roles/review.md`, as a paragraph that gives the user's distinction as the thing to judge by. That paragraph can be corrected like any other passage; what it has to convey is fixed by the user's words above. Whether the other guides that a review session receives agree with it has not been checked by anyone.

### 4.6 Mechanisms the user kept, removed or settled

**Kept, after the over-engineering audit of 24 November.** The audit listed things it thought excessive, and the user answered item by item (session `20538f79`, 13:46:18Z): "1. keep real paper read. 2. review recompile is planned redundancy, it ensures that they compile. 3. compile service feature. 4. intended. 5. intended. 6. fix subreviewer write report stuff. 7. have state file be written once every 10 seconds or on request. 8. if elk is not used and is worse, why is it there? if it really is not needed then clear." The numbers follow the audit's own list. That the first five answers mean that the thing is kept is how the session restated them to the user in its next message, and the user did not object; for the third the user's three words say no more by themselves. The five are: (1) the paper command's eight readings of the manuscript beside real papers; (2) review's own uncached compile; (3) the Lean broker's calibration of memory and its statistical rule for admitting compiles; (4) the `.agents` mirror; (5) the guides as each session receives them. The audit's item spoke of the size of a pack, of one guide that goes to all roles, and of twelve rules stated in several documents (section 3 quotes it in full). The run read the answer, up to its round 1, as keeping both which guides each role receives and the statement of one rule in several documents. On 26 November the user said they were not sure the answer had applied to the restated rules, and gave the way in which repetition is to be restructured (section 3). What stands of this answer is which guides each role receives. A proposal to cut any of these five goes against an explicit decision. Padding in the sense of section 3 was not part of what the audit put to the user. For the third, the audit separately asked whether the broker should exist as a build system of its own beside Lake, and that question the user did not answer (section 4.13).

**Removed, and not to be brought back without a new decision.**
- *Reuse of Lean's state after import ("header snapshots")* in the broker. It was an approved proposal of the agreement; it acquired an exception during implementation, and the user removed it altogether (session `d778f997`, 22 November 04:31:28Z): "그냥 스냅숏을 쓰지 말자 그럼. 예외 경우로 인해 실익이 떨어지면서 복잡성은 추가되는거 같아" (then let's just not use snapshots; the exception case lowers the benefit while adding complexity).
- *The formal judge* (section 4.4).
- *ELK*, an optional layout engine for the dependency map: "쓰이지 않고 더 나쁘면 치우라" as the note records it; the user's words are item 8 above.
- *Report files of sub-reviewers.* A sub-reviewer's report is its final message; item 6 above.

**Settled this week.**
- *The broker writes its state file at most every ten seconds or on request* (item 7 above). The agent that implemented this kept two immediate writes and gave its reasons: one before a compile starts, without which a broker started next could launch a second Lean for the same job, and one after a compile ends, without which a broker started next would finish the job again with a stale measurement of memory. Those two are an assistant's choice, made within the decision as the agent read it.
- *A refuted root is a result.* "루트가 반박으로 끝났으면 그건 루트가 반증되었단 결과를 얻고 끝나야지." (If the root ended in refutation, the research should end with the result that the root was disproved; `d778f997`, 22 November 04:17:56Z.) The dependency map was changed to show it; the particular drawing is the assistant's.
- *Replacing the proof of a settled statement.* The agreement had said that when a better statement supersedes an old one, the old statement's proof is replaced by a one-line derivation from the new statement. That was found to be blocked when the old statement is settled, and the user set the rule: the proof of a settled statement is replaced only by a proof under which the statement stays settled. So the old statement keeps its proof until the new statement is itself settled, because (04:17:56Z) "애시당초 해결되지 않은 새 진술이라면 그게 해결된 진술보다 더 나은 진술일 수 없지 않나?" (a new statement that is not settled cannot be a better statement than a settled one). The four detailed conditions in `runtime/check.py` are the assistant's implementation of it.
- *How long the CLI waits for a session's background work.* Two hours, 7,200,000 ms: "yeah, let's place a limit of 2 hours" (session `0860eb60`, 22 November 20:22:27Z).
- *A publication build that fails for a reason of execution.* A session evaluating the skill reported as its fourth defect that such a failure (a lost host, a restart, a cancellation) is charged to the candidate, which then goes to a replacement peer and a new review, while `reference/operations.md` says the same publication continues. Asked how to dispose of six defects, the user answered for this one "4) pass" (session `3750b5d4`, 22 November 18:29:45Z), beside "apply" for the others. The session took this to mean that the behaviour is left as it is, and the user did not contradict it. Whether the sentence of the document still disagrees with it was not checked; making a document say what the program does is a separate matter and does not decide the behaviour. Changing the behaviour itself would decide what the user's word left standing, and is the user's to decide.
- *Attempts are not held back while a peer or a review waits* (decision after the verification run of 10 November, memory note `M-0698`), and *the operator can post questions with `questions --file`*, which is an official source of questions beside those review admits (decision during the same run).
- *The index extraction runs without Lean's default limit on work* (`set_option maxHeartbeats 0`), approved by the user after a large canon stopped at the limit.
- *The paper does not mention Lean* (section 4.10).
- *The user had the running sessions told not to waste processor and memory on polling* ("tell sessions to not waste cpu and memory on things like polling and stuff", sessions `a494bae3` and `335efa8d`, 22 November 15:48Z to 15:50Z). The changes to waiting described in section 5.2 followed from it.

### 4.7 How a defect is to be repaired

The user has said a good deal this week about what a good repair is, mostly in response to repairs that were not.

**Find the cause and repair that.** Dispatching an agent on an incident, the user asked for "the real, root cause and full solution, not something shallow and surface, something that is simple, effective, and robust, and accurate" (session `0860eb60`, 24 November 11:07:50Z).

**Do not build a safeguard for a state the system should not reach; prevent the state.** The agent's repair for that incident included a path that recovered when a folder the runner needed had been deleted. The user refused it (11:49:12Z):

> "so the issue is that it may be removed, right? and it is a case that should not happen in the first place. I think building a failsafe for that exception is the wrong path to take. the better path is to just avoid it, plain and simple, I think"

The only thing that could delete the folder was the system's own clean-up rule, so the rule was changed and the recovery path, its test and its sentence in the guide were taken out. The note of that session states the lesson as a question to ask of any failure state: who creates this state, and if the system does, repair the rule first.

**Simplicity.** "Simplicity is best." and "If a solution for any problem can be implemented before any runs, at this time now, then just be repeated reliably without much thoughts, that is the best." (session `20538f79`, 24 November 14:32:28Z). That is: a solution that can be put in place once, ahead of time, and then simply relied on is better than one that needs judgment each time it is used. The formal judge was removed on the same ground.

**Delete what is not used.** "delete what is not used" (session `20538f79`, 24 November 11:53:14Z); "delete leftovers." (the same session, 10:40:29Z). The earlier verification runs of this skill, on the user's instruction, treated unused elements and copies of one rule in the program and its tests as damage to remove, and held that "the behaviour is the same" is not by itself a reason to leave them; for repetition in the guides section 3 applies. A file that the program writes into an instance and that nothing reads is a different matter, because removing it changes what a live instance holds: the audit raised three such files, and the user has not answered (section 4.13).

**Repair the instruction, not the agent's freedom.** When agents duplicated work because their briefs told them to, and a session then began building a guard that forbade it, the user said (session `20538f79`, 23 November 01:43:46Z): "you told duplicate workers to do duplicate work, then when they began doing it you tried to add a new ban that would forbid it, when you could simply have told them to just do what they should do in the first place?"

**No compatibility for earlier generations.** The skill does not carry branches for the formats of the third generation and before, migration code or preserved aliases (criterion A4). Section 5.4 describes code that reads what instances of the fourth generation, created earlier this week, still hold. That this code is outside the criterion is this document's reading; it rests on the user having had that code installed together with the removal of the formal judge on 25 November.

**A failure ends the runner; it is not isolated, and nothing restarts the runner.** The program does not pass through an undefined state. A refusal becomes a bounded wait; a failure of the runner's own operation ends the runner with the error recorded, and the operator starts it again after the repair. Isolating the failing piece of work and carrying on, and a watchdog that restarts the runner, are among the directions the user rejected (criteria B5 and B6, and rejected direction R-19 of the criteria file). Sessions that evaluate the skill keep reporting this as a gap; it is a decision.

**The later decision governs** (criterion G4), and a decided matter is not reopened by a correction. When a mechanism the user approved has since acquired exceptions, a repairer who writes a proposal for the user about it should include removing the mechanism among the options; twice this month the user chose a removal that the assistant's options had not offered.

### 4.8 Settings whose values the user chose

The values of these settings are the user's decisions, and a correction that would change one goes to the user: the default number of concurrent sessions; the order of models for each role, including that peer and review prefer a different model from the producing roles; the request for a context window of one million tokens and the threshold at which a session's context is compacted; the limit on a Claude worker's output; the interval between launches of attempts and explorations; the limit on resumptions of a session that ends (`max_end_resumptions`, 3); how often an exploration and an organizer are requested; the two acceptance settings; the two-hour wait for background work; and the default attachment of the literature server in its login-free mode. At commit `162b9e5e` the values, unchanged by the last two commits, are: at most 8 sessions at once; a new attempt or exploration no sooner than 180 seconds after the last; an exploration requested with every attempt launch and an organizer after every 4 publications; the producing roles on `codex/gpt-6.1-sol` and then `claude/opus`, peer and review on `claude/opus` and then `codex/gpt-6.1-sol`, all at the highest effort; a context window of 1,000,000 tokens with compaction at 900,000; an output limit of 128,000 tokens; at most 3 resumptions; both acceptance settings on; 7,200,000 ms of waiting for background work. They stand in `runtime/settings.py` and `runtime/launch.py` and are described in `reference/operations.md`.

### 4.9 The mirror and the running research

The mirror `.agents/skills/do-math` is kept (item 4 of the over-engineering audit). It is produced only by the generator script, never edited by hand (criterion I2); several commits of this week did edit it by hand on the user's instruction while an instance of the earlier generation was running from it, and it was regenerated in full on 23 November at the user's word. Since then commits to do-math regenerate it.

Running research is protected (criterion I1), and it is not paused to make room for other work. When a session paused the sunflower instance to free memory the user had it undone (session `20538f79`, 23 November 09:59:40Z, "undo the pause"). Long runs of a command, such as a paper run, have been started by the user from the user's own terminal; when a session was about to start one and raise its priority, the user said that they would start it themselves (recorded in memory note `M-0765`).

### 4.10 The paper command

The paper command comes from the same brief as the fourth generation (section 4.1). What the user asked of it there (session `d778f997`, 21 November 18:32:49Z):

> "명령 하나로 끝까지, 다만 모든걸 스스로 정하는게 아니라 어떤 방식을 따라야 할지는 사전에 정해져서 스크립트에 의해 동봉. 목표는 root을 해결하는 lean 증명으로부터 … 표준적인 수학 논문의 형식, 방식, 구조, 표현, 작문 … 에 부합하는 방식으로 영어 논문을 생성하는 것. … 구현 세부사항들에 대응해서(예:// 증명 A가 증명 B로 대체시 A는 B로 증명된다로 갈음) 효과적으로 군더더기 없이 깔끔하고 정확하고 간결하게 작성되어야 함(예:// 정리 1. A는 B로 증명 된다. 정리 2. B는 ..., 이런 것은 피해야 해)"

(One command to the end; the command does not decide everything by itself, the way to follow is fixed in advance and supplied by the script. The aim is to produce, from the Lean proof that settles the root, an English paper that conforms to the form, manner, structure, expression and writing of a standard mathematical paper. It is to deal with the details of implementation, for instance by writing that A is proved by B where proof A was replaced by proof B, and to be clean, exact and concise without padding; "Theorem 1. A is proved by B. Theorem 2. B is ..." is the kind of thing to avoid.) The fixed way is `reference/paper-method.md`, which the program hands to its sessions.

The decisions that followed, each in the user's words. Those of 21 and 22 November are from session `d778f997`, those of 23 and 24 November from session `20538f79`:
